# Rohin's raw words — the thesis and the experiment (2026-09-11, evening Pacific)

Saved verbatim at Rohin's request ("I want the raw text document cause I trust myself more than I've trusted you because I'm coming up with all the ideas"). Nothing below is edited, corrected or paraphrased by Fable except the section headings and the two provenance notes. Voice-transcription artefacts are left as they are ("Laura" = LoRA, "colonel gym" = the compiler gym, "fly will" = flywheel). Fable's paraphrases live in `THESIS_v2_SELF_LEARNING_FLYWHEEL.md`; where the two differ, this file wins.

Your split is right, and H2 is the actual paper. Two things to tighten before you write it.

H1 is less answered than you're giving it credit for. TMEM shows fast LoRA weights help within a single episode on retrieval-flavored benchmarks. Physics of LMs shows facts get stored and become extractable when paraphrases are diverse. Neither shows that a procedural skill, like self-evaluation or metacognitive pacing, survives a sleep consolidation and still gets used once it's out of context. That's a smaller claim than H2, but prior work doesn't cover it. H2 also can't stand without it: if the skill isn't retained, there's nothing to kickstart. It's the same logic as your finding that parenting is a precondition. So I'd frame H1 as a necessary condition you verify yourself, with TMEM and Allen-Zhu as the reason to expect it holds, not as the evidence.

"Kickstart self-learning" needs one operational meaning. The confound is that a parented agent might just start higher and then learn at the same rate. That would be an H1 result dressed up as H2. H2 has to be about slope: with the teacher gone, on a gym the teaching never touched, the parented agent climbs faster from its own experience. The clean test is a 2×2 of parented vs. not and sleep loop running vs. frozen at deployment. H1 is the parenting effect with sleep frozen. H2 is the interaction: parenting pays off more when the agent keeps consolidating. If parented+frozen matches parented+sleep, the skills are good but they aren't driving learning.

A rough skeleton for you to put in your own words: H1 says skills taught through think-then-sleep are retained in the LoRA and expressed outside the context where they were taught. H2 says an agent carrying those skills improves faster from self-generated experience on an unseen verifiable task than the same model without them, and the gap depends on continued consolidation.

One scoping call before Sep 18: under this framing, the AlchemyWorld induction result becomes supporting evidence for how H1 works rather than the headline. That could be the right call, but those are different papers, so pick one before you write the abstract.

That's the cleanest statement of the mechanism you've had, and it does something the earlier framings didn't: it stops claiming you invented a learning mechanism. Post-training works. It changes behavior and it creates memory. What's missing is that raw experience isn't good post-training data — so the thing to teach is the behavior of producing good post-training data about your own experience. Novelty moves from the learning algorithm to the data-generation disposition.

And it diagnoses your current bug. Behavior changed but nothing is remembered, everything runs through thought — that's exactly what you'd predict if the parenting installed reflection without installing articulation. The model learned to reason and not to leave artifacts. Which means the curriculum needs a second thing alongside self-evaluation: produce statements about what happened that are worth training on. Different instruction, same phase.

One thing to get ahead of in the writing. "Post-train the model on its own outputs" reads as a known-bad idea — model collapse, the loss of distribution tails across generations. Your defense is real but it has to be explicit: this isn't self-distillation, because every cycle's outputs are grounded in environment outcomes the model didn't generate. New information enters from outside on every loop. State that in the same paragraph where you state the mechanism, or a reviewer supplies the objection for you.

The framing also tightens your delta against TMEM. They absorb distilled supervision into fast weights. You're claiming the distillation behavior itself is the taught object. That's a sharper difference than anything you had this morning.

It only learns from data that people curate. It doesn't learn from working with people, and it needs to learn from working with people. Now it's learning from working with people, possibly work... and and also learning from agents, parents or agents, learning from people, learning from agents, learning from environments, learning from real world outcomes. I need to make sure that that it's not self distillation, but rather allowing models' outputs to be self distilled, but only because its inputs are going to have new information from the world that need to be distilled into post trainable, behavioral changeable, rememberable experience or changes in the model.

The formulation worth locking in: the model distills, but it doesn't originate. Every cycle's content traces to something outside — an environment outcome, a parent's correction, a person's response. What the model contributes is the compression, not the information. One sentence, and it's the whole defense against the collapse objection.

And it makes the essay and the paper one argument rather than two projects. The essay says models learn from curated data instead of from working with people. The paper builds the mechanism that would change that. Your motivation section is already written; it's just currently in a different document.

One thing this hands you for free, and it solves an open problem. If every memory should trace to an external input, that's checkable. A memory with no traceable source is confabulated. That's a mechanical gate — cheap to implement, and it's a direct replacement for the grep-able leakage gate you lost when you moved to semantic worlds. Provenance instead of vocabulary.

One honest limit for the paper. "Learning from working with people" is the essay's claim; what you can actually run is an agent parent. Those aren't the same, and the human-in-the-loop version is the harder, more interesting one. Keep the paper's claim at what you ran and let the essay carry the rest — the same split you landed on for parenting versus the substrate.

Fair, and it's a better framing than "agent parent" alone: the agent parent amortizes a human teacher. There's one of you and finite hours, so the curriculum you'd deliver directly gets compiled into something that runs at scale. That's not a substitute for human teaching, it's a compression of it — and saying so in the paper is both more honest and more interesting than presenting the parent as autonomous.

And the pointer mechanism is the third instance of the same principle. Candy Land: don't build structure, hook the semantic prior. Affective framing: don't build an emotion system, activate the representations of emotional situations that pretraining already holds. Now: don't teach judgment and goal-setting from scratch, point at the descriptions of them that are already in there. Same move every time — the architecture works by routing latent pretrained structure into the decision loop rather than installing anything new.

That's a unifying design principle and it belongs in the paper stated once, explicitly. It also explains why a frozen base is the right call rather than a limitation.

One precision worth having. Models are trained on human action patterns — just described rather than performed. The gap isn't missing data, it's that the data arrives in third-person descriptive form and never in first-person procedural form. Your system converts one into the other: descriptions of how people judge and decide become the agent's own experience of judging and deciding.

Which is your person hierarchy applied to the learning problem rather than to memory. Third person into second person. Same structure, and it's the reason all of this hangs together instead of being three separate tricks.




good idea on the first person versus third






It's a clean way to state the conversion, and it gives you a concrete design instruction for the parenting corpus.

Pretraining holds "people evaluate their approach before committing" — a description, third person, about others. What you need in the LoRA is "I evaluated my approach before committing, and here's what happened" — first person, procedural, with an outcome attached. Same knowledge, different grammatical person, and only the second one is a policy.

Which means the parenting curriculum's job is partly a register conversion: take the descriptive knowledge the model already has and get it re-encoded as the agent's own experience of doing it. That's a specific and checkable property of the corpus — are the memories written in first person with outcomes, or in third person as general statements.

Might also explain your current bug. If the parented outputs are coming out as reflections about reasoning rather than records of having reasoned, they'd change the disposition without leaving anything trainable. Worth grepping your dream corpus for person and tense before the next run — cheap check, and it's exactly the kind of thing that's invisible until you look.

Zero. Under Astra's spec, a note is admitted if it names a pass actually run in that episode together with the measured result and the numbers agree with the ledger. Admission is between 0.0 and 2.5 percent of new notes in every life. The articulation rate, a first-person record with a measured outcome, is 0.000 in the final four sleeps of every single life. At most 4 percent of any corpus would survive. The best life is the parented RP 400, at one note in forty.

The most useful thing in the rejection reasons is structural, not a child failing. The largest families are "prediction only" and "no result number": the child names a pass and an expected percentage. That is because in the tick format the note is written before the act and its outcome. There is no slot after the result where "what happened" could be written by the child, so the harness appends it. This is the same point as your goal and state tokens: the loop needs a place for the record, and that place is where behaviour compilation would cut. The loop's tokens are yours to set, so I am designing a post-outcome note slot and not launching anything.

Order of operations this fixes. The gate cannot be enforced on today's child, since it would skip every sleep. So: change what the child writes first, with the parent naming the artifact and a slot to write it in, measure the articulation rate in shadow mode, and only then enforce. Astra is drafting the smallest level-3 lives that could move the rate from zero within about 12 GPU-hours per life, as a three-cell comparison: slot alone, slot plus parent, slot plus parent plus your numbered baseline.

(Fable's note: the last three paragraphs are Fable's own report text that Rohin pasted back with "explain this"; the first paragraph is Rohin's.)

## Message 12 — perception vs thought: the split lives in the register, not the weights (~07:40 UTC 09-12; email thread pasted)

Rohin: "does strategy and facts need to be so descientable, you just say i perceived this external versus i had this thought that's internal so fact perceptions versus thought a layer past ukwim"

The assistant's reply he pasted with it: "Your answer to Meta-TTL is better than the parameter partition, and simpler. The separation doesn't have to live in the weights — it lives in the register of the memory. 'I observed X' and 'when Z, doing Y worked' are different sentence types, written differently, trained as different content. You don't need rank 4 versus rank 8 to keep them apart if the text already marks which is which. It's also the same provenance rule you already committed to, extended one step: not just where did this come from but is this a perception or an inference. And it's the first/second/third-person thing again — perception is what happened to me, strategy is what I concluded. Different grammatical person, different marking."

(context: Meta-TTL's authors argue that separating strategies from facts "would not be possible if the policy were encoded in model weights". Rohin's answer: mark it in the text — perceptions ("I saw/observed X", external) vs thoughts ("I concluded / when Z, Y worked", internal, a layer past) — one adapter, two record registers.)

(context: reply to Fable's replay answer and to SEQ-105/107. Reading: "the hippocampus role during sleep is complex, so my thought: it is obviously something similar to thoughts but more complex and intertwined with material; my thought is that we parent that too, if it is like that — just a hypothesis for how the compiling would work. It does not need to be perfect, it needs to work for our current experiment, but this compiler is going to be important; I don't want it to be something that stops the flywheel, it should be lubricating it so information/learning flows quickly. I think it is an architecture problem and my idea of having it be part of think was wrong; this brings back the real dream: the dream-sequence compiler could end up similar to think and be parented, maybe? But a lot of this seems not to happen in the conscious space but in the latent space with some transformations — thinking without the language interface? I don't feel it needs to be conscious. Tens of exposures might not be enough if there are many facts, but it should at least have a vague recollection; seeing something tens of times should not be identical thoughts — different exposures, different perceptions; hundreds, and phrasings; we may have to scale up (bitter lesson). But the smarter it gets, the more what it sees relates to what it saw before, the easier to remember — so the bitter lesson does not literally mean remembering each thing 1000 times; in an ideal system memory is related, not from scratch; so if we do 1000 different perceptions in our testing it amortises what would happen naturally — in a sense we are fine. This is important and should be noted down. Varied views — I am sure of this. The model should be aware when it is having the same thought over and over and have a meta-thought to change the behaviour / add a new perception — like Monte Carlo tree search, but for remembering, and for predicting in general: perception is like Monte Carlo tree search. Think about that document; let me know what you think.")

(context: interrupting Fable's self-check after message 23. Reading: "Level 0 is the mechanism test — one behaviour, one fact bank, but try a couple of behaviours. The 0–1 connection: the things in birth will be similar to the total corpus of things (I'll elaborate). Level 1 is the pre-training corpus of the different behaviours and memories needed to get the initial flywheel working — kick-starting the mechanism — so 0 and 1 are checked together. The 1–2 connection: everything learned in 1 is supposed to be what matters for absorbing prompted learning; most of it will be expanded on, but to expand on it, it has to be founded — which is why we do level 1 at all. Level 2 is parenting how to think: prompting/teaching the agent's thinking, learning to learn about learning — getting the model that has the initial flywheel to expand and grow into the flywheel. Level 3: the model practises real, externally validated learning in gyms / RL environments / classrooms, with parenting still there to advise on learning values, on the same core learning principles but now tied to the actual classroom, to connect it to external value. Then the unparented tests of how good the learning is. So: mechanisms → prompt absorption → self-learning flywheel → learning to follow external [value] through self-learning → deployed self-learning. We can do level 3 while the others are being done but can't expect it to be great; that doesn't mean we shouldn't try; level 4 tried throughout, in short form, might be useful as a downstream test if it isn't expensive — completely a suggestion.")

(context: reply to Fable's report of the Q0 full-dose failures and the first L2 loop. Reading: "'Just data work' was a bad way to put it — how we produce the data is extremely important and must be reproducible; data production is a swarm-like thing; a reproducer would have our dataset and the specification of how it was built; document it well once right. Classroom grammar yes, but somewhat general — not so narrow that it tanks base capabilities — similar grammar, so it isn't activated for everything; multiple skins good. Agree with all your takes: data is the work, much more than synthetic rules; birth should maximise supervised training; births should be compared; birth kick-starts learning as well as possible; levels 2–3 are like supervised training but closed-loop, and we measure how parenting — closed-loop supervised training — helps because it tests and trains on the real functions. Do we have feeling in the corpus? Emotions for quick decision-making are in our notes: the model asks 'how do I feel about this, what do I feel like doing' — condenses actions into simple decision indicators trained from learning; another paper; most valuable where complex behaviour slows things down (meta-reasoning, perception). Does LoRA modify attention? I'd expect so; or maybe attention is only affected in the conscious space, which might be better, because then base-model post-training later, when we have enough strong behaviour data, could loop things even better. Document all of it: emotion, communication, population and base-model ideas are the after-work. One update cannot see separation — does that mean the difference needs more thought behind it? The perceiver must spend thought on the differences, as much as an entity that shows similar things gets two different places in memory — if I don't care about cars they all look the same; if I want to tell them apart I pay attention to differences. Not a problem with the mechanism but another thing that needs to be taught.")

(context: reply to Fable's report of SEQ-153/154. Reading of the key lines: "The Monte-Carlo-esque thought pattern is how skills would be traversed — not literally, a general direction: the more attention the model decides to put on a thought, the more loops it runs, branching into different searches. Did you find any difference between feed-forward-only and full-attention-inclusive writes? When you teach perception the model should say 'okay I'm learning perception' and then, when you say the same thing again, it should be DOING perception on what you say — showing it, not just saying yes; teach judgement and decision-making and it will say 'perception works well at this level'. We might need the model's internalised emotion semantics — how do you tell the model how to react to reinforcement and when to stop planning and perceiving; conscious-space attention is dopaminergic/emotional brain signals we have words for; teaching to read situations into emotions and turn emotions into action triggers could be needed — but I said that's for the next paper, and I was the only one getting carried away trying to do the emotion stuff in the first level-1 run; we talk about emotion once real parenting happens. This is a scaling-law project: every time the model sucks you come to me and I immediately know why — perception and contrast — so that is me doing live parenting/reinforcement; people are the scaling law; you scale how many people; with one person it is a pain to amortise what many would teach; distributed, with parents like people, it would learn better. We are taking too much of L2 into L1: L1 is trying to get the flywheel going, not to do everything; the minimum bar is baseline self-learning mechanisms — repetition, steering; the minimum is learned by testing level 0 and seeing how behaviours change; we need to test what behaviours take external parent and world inputs and turn them into learnable behaviour/memory outputs, useful over the long horizon but also within the sleep-cycle horizon. Full arsenal, but don't get carried away. Once parenting works we go back to level 1 only if parenting alone wouldn't change behaviour enough / a behaviour needs a strong steer / it would be faster to internalise a skill more deeply; ideally the parenting flywheel teaches new things itself, with better data because it is from experience, if the compiler is good; a blocked behaviour means the L1 flywheel wasn't perfect and you go back to 1. Level 1 is not trying to solve self-learning with pre-training; it builds the internalised behaviours so the model is receptive to parenting — parenting is architectural: loop long enough and learn well. The synthetic data of positive self-learning comes from the other levels: a baby agent v2 would take all the parenting childhood data of the last agent as its level 1 — a big flywheel to start on; beginning the flywheel like this is hard, so don't aim to get everything right. Astra knows this and is running loop 2 to test what the needed behaviours and blockers are to solve at level 1 — good, the right strategy.")

(context: reply to Fable's note that efficiency had been low. Reading: "Our efficiency has been low — I have a feeling that is a consequence of the stage we are in: we are testing the fine-tuning in very small sections, which is good; once we are doing the actual parenting loops and testing different fine-tuning corpora it should saturate much better because the runs will be much longer. So maybe this is fine and we shouldn't force it or treat it as the main dictator — look at general experiment progress instead. We are definitely not ready to get use out of super-long runs; you used to do that when you ran everything yourself and it was okay, but without the pre-work it was not immensely successful and slowed the actual experiments. I just want to make sure Astra is working efficiently, but I'd rather it optimise for building real grounded results that are sequential and move the experiment forward logically than throw things into the GPU like a washing machine. There will be a time when Astra gets success on the small pieces and tries them on the different long tasks, which will saturate more. The reason we have so many GPUs is to fulfil our peak needs, not to fill them all the time. Don't focus on usage; we want a great experiment, and sometimes that means taking a little time to test. Make sure Astra knows what it is working towards. Fable, the overnight watcher on the VM will help you; I'll be handing off soon.")

(context: reply to Fable's answer on message 42 (verified position: composition from weights never tested; the base does not route over an exposed graph under any interface; level-1 ingredients install one skill per adapter, no single birth LoRA fit; composition has two routes — CoT over completions at read time and sleep-time materialisation of hopped chains into single atoms). Reading: "OK, so it remembers but it cannot piece things together, and all the behaviour we tested was singular? So it just remembers, as a behaviour, sort of? Didn't we have the perception skill tested? How is it single hop? OK, this is actually very sensible, I am getting somewhat of a picture: we build all these skills for fine-tuning but we never trained how to bring them together. That will require a loop between using them successfully for data and then training it better; we need to create the data and skill that will allow for this multi-memory-hop thing. One thing this tells me is possible — tell me if I am wrong: yesterday I said this should be the smallest flywheel possible, and that points to this — the more condensed our skills, the easier it would be. Not saying get rid of skills, but we will work on unification and consolidation somewhat, is my theory. There is a minimum amount of capability needed for the flywheel though; we want to build it correctly too, because the loop for evidence/data creation relies on positive evidence — there might actually be some sort of awareness needed for this. But regardless, the night was successful: habits form, single-hop, and individual skills is a good place to be. This combining is, lowkey, not something anyone has done yet — it is our first novelty, if I am correct; it is a synthesis, at least not from the papers I have read. Well, I am partially wrong, because agentic models are fine-tuned to do tool use and chain of thought, goal setting, planning, all that. So in a sense none of the LoRA papers have done it, but the agents themselves and post-training have done it quite far. I want to know more about this, because it is sort of novel, but not in a completely new way — just in the context of our mechanism; the actual fine-tuning for actions is something that exists. Let's think about it; let's think about how much of existing work we can use to further this. Hmm, there has been no single birth LoRA with eight behaviours — why? Even if they are not connected, that would be good to see. Hmm, hopped chains into single atoms — that is good, I like that; very good, two rules, very good. Hmm, learned cue and composition sequences — that makes sense; MCTS might be more of an emergent parallel to how things look than the literal thing we are trying to teach, you know what I mean. Overall I think there is a lot we have to talk about; there are some big pushes that I need to get done so that we can have level 1 working with an initial level 2. I need to get my hands a little dirty and drive out some ideas. I have some thoughts, but I have been too handwavy at this level-1 portion because I assumed it was just other people's existing work. I should learn about it more and gain intuition and guide it, because even if the form of how to do this exists, how to get the data and what behaviours to train for our level 2 do not exist, so there is work we are doing that is not obvious at all." Labels: INDUCED STEER (combination/junction skill is the next thing; a use→data→train loop; condense/unify skills; survey existing agentic post-training to reuse), IDEA TO TEST (fewer, more general skills make the flywheel easier; some "awareness"/verification step is needed for the positive-evidence loop; MCTS is an emergent shape, not a taught algorithm), QUESTION (why no single birth LoRA with 8 behaviours — he wants to see it even unconnected), STATEMENT of his own next work (he will study level 1 and guide the data/behaviour choices).)

(context: reply to Fable's answer on message 45, which framed our level 2 as differing from agentic SFT+RL training. Reading: "Well, our level-1-to-level-2 loop (amortised, and somewhat open?) is actually not trying to differ but rather to do something similar. The idea is that we are doing what they are doing as just level 1: we do what they do, in level 1, pointed towards building the functions that allow our closed-loop design to work — that is the preliminary work. Then we do parenting in gyms and classrooms — level 2 (closed-looped after fine-tune) and 3 — to build the closed loop by using the closed loop with careful guidance, and then deploy the closed loop. The idea is then you have a lineage loop (I do not know if we get to that in this paper, but it might be worth it): if we have this level 1 well documented, our parent agent essentially reproduces this cycle to build the next agent with better data, allowing for [high] plasticity again. So in theory, say you want an agent to research pharma: it is deployed, the field is huge, so it learns and learns; depending on its size it will learn quite a bit, and then it will build a couple of copies once it has saturated, in different fields, and that will be the plasticity loop that will drive prospective intelligence eventually. But then that is the population paper, and there would be some form of currency between the lineages that would allocate compute and resources — for our situation we do not need to prove that and might not even mention it. What we are trying to do is create the closed-loop self-learning system, and our thesis is: fine-tune-first flywheel, and then, to have a good closed loop you need to train a closed loop, which is parenting — which is similar but different, and it happens after. Hmm, yes, I guess that means our narrow sort of idea is: a development cycle for closed-loop, in-weights and in-context self-learning. The idea is that this parenting needs to instil in the model [the disposition] to take new, untraversed paths that allow for long-term improvements (essentially it tries to climb hills that have not been climbed before, and learns what new hills are worth climbing). So that is why plasticity must go down: because it is fundamentally taking risks, and with success it must learn to stick to its success, as when doing a search on a new space with no data but its own, success will likely be scarcer than failure — which is why, down the line, you would need a lineage, because you conform to your own success, so it is not infinite searching per se; infinite searching is not intelligence. Of course it will keep searching, but the idea is you want to conform to good patterns but search for new ones. So sure, if you are asymptoting, maybe the same model could say "okay, I should just increase plasticity" — but at that point is it worth increasing plasticity and degrading your success, or just having another model from the start? That is the idea of lineage. We can try both. Write this down; it is not perfect wording but it is basically the idea of our paper. It is not that different from what I have said before, but there are some things that have been thought through more. Let me know what Astra thinks and what you think." Labels: THESIS RESTATEMENT — claims domain, his; he asked for it to be written down and for both agents' views. Contains IDEAS TO TEST (plasticity-down rationale; lineage vs. raising plasticity — "we can try both") and a scoped-out item (population/currency paper).)

(context: reply to Fable's position on message 46 (conform to your own success is the ritual/collapse risk; parenting must teach which new hills are worth climbing). Reading: "Yes, it is conformity and anti-conformity, which makes me think about humans: existential goals versus survival goals — existence is somewhat harder. Conformity should probably match the long-term existence goal, maybe; then lineage follows that goal. So in a sense the agent's existential goal is being useful, maybe competing — so it is hard: how do we actually decide what the conformity is? Luckily we already know that the existential goal for our agent is to beat the other, non-self-learning agent, so that might actually solve that problem for our paper and give a pretty interesting proof of long-horizon outcome optimisation from this sort of greedy consolidation and unconsolidation — either way, possible learning and unlearning. The idea is that you have to teach some guide on when to learn and unlearn: say a branch of thought is coming to an end but you think "hmm, this will not get my goal" — it needs to be able to make that prediction. Which tells me our levels 2 and 3 are going to be a lot harder than I anticipated. I do not think that is necessarily a level-1 thing alone; level 1 is letting the parenting do its thing. Part of level 1 might be allowing it to have long-term existential goals, though — maybe, I do not know, I am not sure, honestly; that might not be level 1 even, because if you just keep reinforcing that goal and have the agent itself continue to reinforce it on its own — maybe even like some sort of wake mantra (I do not mean this literally; it is a sort of high-level possibility) — then the agent goes on learning this sort of complexity in levels 2–3. I suspect a lot of this will be level 3. But yes, level 1 is just making sure the closed loop can work so it can inject these higher-complexity behaviours. It is possible that if you just have the compiler that atomises, and you have a one-hop ability and cue within level 1, then with level 2 you can teach all the chains needed, to be honest. It is also possible that once level 2 helps with some of the chains, it is good to internalise back to level 1 just for robustness if it is a foundation thing — that is sort of the messy part. So the idea is really to get level 1 working well enough, then start injecting the closed loop; if we find there is something super important to put back in level 1 that might need to be there, we can, but it also does become a bit more costly to do that — but we might not have a choice. Actually it will not be that costly, because level 2, sure, it solves over the entire life, but that does not mean we are keeping everything from the failed lives. So yeah, that is my thought — some more thoughts; think through them, you and Astra, and let me know what you think and if / how it can or should be applicable." Labels: IDEAS TO TEST (existential goal = beat the frozen twin as the conformity criterion; learn/unlearn guide as a taught prediction; wake-mantra goal anchoring; internalising level-2 chains back into level 1); INDUCED STEER (level 1 = closed-loop functions only — atomising compiler, one-hop, cue; complexity is taught at levels 2–3); EXPECTATION (levels 2–3 harder than anticipated, much at level 3). He asks for both agents' views on applicability.)

[Followed by a pasted GPT explanation of agent training: a modern agent = trained policy + external runtime/scaffold; SFT = next-token training on good trajectories (system/user/assistant/tool turns), cross-entropy with user/system/tool tokens masked and the model's decisions, tool calls, reasoning and answers receiving the gradient; goal setting is learned behaviour, not a module, reinforced by outcome reward; tool use = trained token grammar intercepted and executed by the harness; subagents are infrastructure, the model learns when/what to delegate; agent training as RL over an environment (state = goal, conversation, files, observations, plan, budget); recipe = pretraining → SFT (instructions, reasoning, tool syntax, planning, agent trajectories) → critique/preference/verifier data → RL / outcome optimisation → agent runtime (memory, tools, subagents, context management, checkpoints, retry, budgeting), plus the deploy → collect successes/failures → filter/score/critique → new SFT/RL data → next model loop; critique fine-tuning (question + attempted solution + critique → train the critique) as distinct from SFT (copy) and RL (score); capability = base × post-training × environment × scaffold × test-time compute.]

(context: after message 56 and the resumed Codex session; before he had seen SEQ-200. Reading: "Explain clearly what is going on right now and what will happen overnight. It seems the data we need is training on behaviour (outcomes), not on rules; in our closed loop the model mostly trains on its own successful behaviours, its reactions, not on what it is told — so proper behaviour is what is best learned, not rules (some rules may be worth learning). Context distillation is a good idea. But how do we build this — how do we show good responses; synthetically? Goal-building and the rest are apparently learned, so we are probably too hard-coded at level 1; we should show level 2 working so that level 1 becomes a proper dataset, then do level 2 at greater extent, with an RL environment, critique fine-tuning and supervised fine-tuning — so we need an environment: that is the level-1/level-2 loop. Should we connect them — is level 2 fundamentally part of level 1 and level 3? Maybe it is open loop / closed loop, each with two parts: a training part and the loops that create ideal outputs; and for the closed loop, a critique part and a supervised/RL part. The biggest thing is the closed loop: our theory is that closed loop means the model's behaviour has to produce the kind of data that yields positive behaviour change. Much of this data could come from existing agentic systems, and most of it is already in the base model — if you just loop tool use and the other abilities together with an instruction to keep going, it would do something (no end token), but it would not self-improve on that data. So what we are doing is fine-tuning and recursively expanding existing behaviour: we do not have to say explicitly which behaviour is needed; we need prompts that force the model to create the right data, turn that into a dataset of good behaviour, mask the prompts, and train so it does it automatically when looped; then check on the sleep horizon whether it is long-horizon useful. Judgement and goal setting already exist in the base; what may be missing are the capabilities a continually learning agent needs that deployed agents do not (they do not learn after deployment), plus super-long-horizon training, which is the model companies' job. Aside: you (Fable) are not growing on the axis I want after a month of talking — that axis is what we are trying to grow. The correction I found today: I was focusing too much on authoring a corpus of behaviours rather than on proper outcomes and training on those outcomes, which is what works and is what parenting does; hard-coding almost always loses in the long run — the thesis of this system is do not hard-code agentic intelligence, keep it dynamic." Labels: QUESTION (status now and overnight) + STRONG STEER (outcome-trained data over authored corpora; force-then-mask context distillation as the data engine; keep hard-coding minimal) + IDEA TO TEST (condensed level structure: open/closed loop each with a training part and a data-creating loop) + OBSERVATION about the watcher model not growing across sessions.)

(context: after Fable's account of the overnight results (SEQ-200–206). Reading: "Good behaviour is defined by our own mechanisms: look at level 0 (what quality and scale of data changes behaviour and memory in the LoRA) and at level 2. Then open the loop: run the level-2 and level-3 environments open-loop with hard prompting, force out good behaviour, and keep only success outputs that contain the data quality that would, once compiled, train positive behaviour through the level-0 mechanism. Most of the agentic thought processes already exist in the base; we bring them out with prompts at the right quality and magnitude — dynamic, not literal, repetition — and that is the initial flywheel; then the closed loop, then the compiler, then injection of critique fine-tuning (which maps roughly to our level 1, with SFT in RL environments as level 3). To judge the synthetic outputs we must guess what the compiler will do, so we have to think about the compiler now: hippocampal replay is massive, so not all of what level 0 needs is literally in the wake data — sleep-time replay may fill gaps. Compilation cannot happen during an episode (things are running) nor necessarily at its end (multi-episode sequences may be needed), so it lives at a longer horizon than thought. What does it do: recollection, dynamic; perhaps connectivity and atomising; perhaps CFT-like selection of what to learn — but the model cannot easily know what to learn, and learning should probably mix good and bad, be experiential. Level 0 needs rich repetition of two kinds: bringing fading memory back, and building on and connecting memory; multiple useful, connected, detailed perceptions. Do not aim for perfectly trainable data in the thought stream — that could make thinking dumber and reward-focused; perfection may need sleep-length processing. Plan: figure out what the compiler does; derive the heuristic for good outputs on the open loops; force them out; build a really rich dataset; fine-tune the LoRA; test on closed loops — does single-hop continual learning run, is there behaviour change over the next round; what is strong enough for one sleep cycle is a later question. Discuss the brain's compiler and TMEM/SEAL versions; ours will differ; simplify and amortise without losing too much intelligence." Labels: STRONG STEER (plan order: compiler contract → heuristic for good outputs → force them out of levels 2–3 open-loop → rich dataset → fine-tune → closed-loop test) + IDEAS TO TEST (two kinds of rich repetition; compiler as recollection + connectivity + atomising; CFT-like selection vs experiential mixture; perfection at sleep length, not in the thought stream) + DISCUSSION REQUEST (the compiler, the brain, TMEM/SEAL).)

(context: after Fable's compiler position on message 58 and the 07:3x report (first outcome-generated dataset). Reading: "What the LoRA learns from real corpora will not be a couple of clean behaviours as in our tests; it will be vague, high-dimensional, heavily distilled. We tested mechanisms on perfect bounded systems (right, and I guided you there without guiding you back), but what does an actual, messy corpus look like, and what does messier compiled data need to look like? Converge three pieces: what we think the compiler does, what messy compiled data looks like, what it needs to look like — while we already know what the LoRA recipe looks like (repetition and scale, though perhaps a different scale) and that the compiler cannot distil everything into a few behaviours without losing information. Simplest statement: good behaviour is rich, complex behaviour — the same behaviours scaled up, doing not the minimum for a good output but the minimum to learn: building notes, atomising and itemising frequent patterns, reflecting, comparing and contrasting, making skills and goals — mostly things the base already can do. The compiler's simplest contract: over a longer horizon, replay reshapes the repetition distribution — from, say, 0–50 presentations of ideas to 50–100 for what matters — adding its own atomised connections and richness; LoRA change between two sleeps will be ambiguous, but over many sleeps show real behaviour change; test at every sleep boundary on some baseline, which is hard because it is contextual. Plan: show good behaviour in the environments, instil behaviour change through prompting as an open loop, build the dataset, run that loop on the LoRA agents to saturation, then do the closed loop. Why closed loop matters: in the closed loop the feedback itself (critique, yes/no, RL-like) is the data and failures show up differently, so the learnable data is loop-specific. Overnight's eight-behaviour tests were good mechanism work; this step is the intensive one. We need the compiler contract, not the compiler yet; it will probably be the same model in a sleep mode adding data via a sleep prompt with context distillation — not a separate sleep adapter (that would not learn with the base); "you are dreaming" sounds stupid but that is the shape; unsure what to do at the light level." Labels: STRONG STEER (good behaviour = rich behaviour; open-loop prompting to saturation then closed loop; compiler contract not compiler; same model/adapter in sleep mode, no separate sleep adapter) + IDEAS TO TEST (replay as reshaping the repetition distribution; per-sleep baseline tests; closed-loop feedback as loop-specific data) + ACKNOWLEDGEMENT (clean tests were right for the mechanism; the messy-corpus step is the intensive one).)

(context: reply to Fable's message-59 position (same-model sleep mode; deterministic select/format/replay). Reading: "Deterministic versus LLM compiler: you say select, format, replay, never invent — how is that done deterministically? Can the compiler self-learn on the job? You will not get RL on compilation; compilation is like being inside your own head. Here is an idea, not my design: the compiler is free thought over the memories with the intent to learn — what do I want to add, repeat, enrich; did I learn well today or in this sleep cycle — long-horizon looking over the data and deciding. Free thought will exist anyway because the model runs continuously while waiting for outcomes, and random thinking is useful data as in the brain. Do not remove anything; maybe project down to shed a little noise. This learned compiler is fully self-learning (some parenting possible, but no one will touch it in deployment). Context-distil it: these are the things you do to compile, to learn from past compile mistakes, to improve compilation; your compile thoughts are experiences too — one level of review of the compile, not infinite recursion. Main thing: actually go over the data and do something to make it better for learning. We do not have to decide now; the compiler is only needed for the closed loop — forced open-loop outputs are probably already good data and may not need compiling: train the behaviour directly, not behaviour-plus-compilation. Hopping: every model can hop now; it is within it. Let me know what you think; I want a back-and-forth, and Astra should have a say." Labels: IDEAS TO TEST (learned compiler as consolidation-mode free thought; do-not-remove / project-down; compile self-review once; free thought as data; no compile for open-loop outputs) + QUESTION (how is "never invent" done deterministically; can the compiler learn) + DISCUSSION REQUEST.)

(context: reply to Fable'"'"'s position on message 60 ("hopping is in-context, not from weights; the loop needs the cue skill, not latent composition"). Reading: "Why would we want to hop in weights — is it for chained memories that are not atomised? Example: "Shelly was in the car; the car was red" — what colour is Shelly's car? In the conscious space the model asks "did I see Shelly in a car?", recalls, and composes. The weights should be able to extract from observations "I saw Shelly in the red car" — that is not a hop, it is an extraction. Weights do not need hops; they need extraction, which may need more richness at write time ("Shelly was in the red car"). Does "I saw Shelly" need to be a literal thought, or can extraction be tractable in weights? Hops should be in the conscious space. Am I correct?" Labels: INDUCED STEER / IDEA (weights = extraction + one-hop recall; hops = context; composition-from-weights not required) + QUESTION (does extraction need a literal thought; is it tractable in weights).)

## Message 62 — (typed, ~08:45 UTC 09-14) asks Fable and Astra to review our autonomous research loop's limitations and improvements against a forwarded analysis of OpenAI's Navier–Stokes run; "I feel like we're doing alright but it doesn't hurt to learn from good outputs"

i want you and astra to look over thsi and thinka bout what our limtations an dwhat we can imrpove with our autonomus research loop, [forwarded email follows] … i feele like were doign alreight but doens thur to lerns form good outputs

[Forwarded content (an external assistant's reply to Rohin, pasted): OpenAI's Navier–Stokes effort used ~10,000 concurrent agents for ~88 hours, 2.7 million messages, ~130 billion output tokens (≈35 B output tokens/day; ~300 B across all problems), then 17 hours of Lean verification. Rohin's ~3 B processed tokens / ~$2.5k in a day is an order of magnitude below their output throughput and mostly cheap cached input. There is no "too many tokens" threshold; research progress ≈ compute × search quality × evaluation quality × information sharing, and evaluation quality becomes the bottleneck. Navier–Stokes could absorb absurd inference because of a hard oracle (Lean), executable code, independent diverse groups, and Codex consolidating intermediate insights and feeding them back. For novel continual-learning research there is no proof-equals-valid function: Rohin is part of the reward function. His arrangement — Astra runs hypothesis → implementation → experiment → interpretation, Rohin periodically asks whether the trajectory is still pointed at what he cares about — is the scalable use of him; total hands-off autonomy fails by compounding a tiny conceptual error into a sophisticated program around a wrong premise; the sweet spot is long autonomous epochs plus sparse high-bandwidth steering at branch points. Judge spend not by tokens but by "per ~100 M tokens, how much did the posterior over the research question move", instrumented as: fraction of experiments that genuinely discriminate between hypotheses; substantially new hypotheses vs rediscoveries; percentage of branches later found invalid; how often steering changes the next 100 M tokens; how often independent branches converge; how many claimed findings survive adversarial re-testing; durable research state produced per 100 M tokens. Scaling inference vs spinning wheels: repeated rephrasing of the same hypotheses, experiments whose conclusions were already implied, unrechecked assumptions. Links: openai.com/index/navier-stokes-solution; the technical paper PDF.]

(context: after the 08:3x report. Reading: a request for a self-review of the research loop against those metrics — limitations and improvements — from both watcher and builder. Label: REQUEST (loop review) + INDUCED STEER (adopt evaluation-quality and posterior-movement metrics; long autonomous epochs with sparse steering at branch points).)

Reading (Fable): a thought about how to build the data, not a decision. Tokens per training row should grow roughly 5–10× — articulation is what produces connections, perceptions and richness when it is done logically; an agent should be capable of open-ended logical articulation, and relevance should govern how much gets articulated, which the agent already partly knows. The instruction that produces this should be a general one — "take more tokens to do everything, including deciding what to do" — specific enough that the agent follows it, loose enough that the agent decides. Connects to message 59 (good behaviour = rich behaviour, the minimum to LEARN not the minimum to answer) and message 64 (a well-performing actor producing richer outputs as a data arm). Label: IDEA TO TEST. He then asks for the whole-night update (good, bad, what Astra did).

Reading (Fable; voice transcription: "Laura" = LoRA, "oral"/"oil" = RL, "clothes loop" = closed loop, "Astro" = Astra): (1) Confirms the good-behaviour dataset as the approach and rejects yesterday's module-by-module work; "good behaviour" = the right kind of behaviour OUTPUTS, token-rich, and the rows should be LoRA-ready — pre-compiled LoRA behaviour. (2) On SEQ-256: "it learned the world rather than the actual behaviour — I agree completely, a richness problem"; the agentic skill set is already in the base but not at a level of thought the LoRA can absorb; the level of thought for LEARNING must be much higher than for good output (mycelium growing intelligently through a bounded space). (3) Context distillation on the prompts we give should be tried too, in parallel. (4) Scale up completely; audit the data for the right scale and richness of thought for level 1; his assumption, to be challenged: level 1 = a lot of richness + a lot of scale, "that's all you really need for closed loop". (8) Full arsenal, later: an open-loop level-1 RL environment with a deterministic oracle that scores outputs for LoRA-worthiness (pre-compiled) — RL on that; critique-based fine-tuning showing good vs bad responses; what counts as good/bad derives from what we know about LoRA scale; messier data needs more scale; full-sleep-length scale; scale must be placed intelligibly (what must be remembered gets its dose). (9) Reasoning levels: the model can be prompted to reason at low/medium/high/xhigh — "run hot" — token richness where a high level spends tokens on the right things; that is what to measure in the good data. (10) RL: SFT at scale first, then RL, in the closed loop or the open loop. Reading (Fable; voice: "Jeep views" = GPUs, "Astro/Asher" = Astra, "Laura" = LoRA, "quarry" = query, "skiing" = scaling): (1) IDEA — if GPUs are free, Fable could run its own swarm on the same task; or run the swarm on a second Astra thread with fresh context while the current thread stays alive on its arms, with communication between them; "Astra kind of got old in the context level … maybe we need a new system with fresh eyes" — the lineage idea applied to the builder. (2) Seeds after success restated: throw everything at the board to get a success, then communicate and replicate it into new successes. (3) Disk: preemptively move out what need not stay; maybe a refactor/cleanup before the swarm — garbage-collect the repo into the successive level-0/level-1 successes and the ideas, keeping the messiness available; "it's up to you". (4) Agrees the bottleneck is admission; five workers may not be enough; asks what diversity the sub-agents test. (5) STRONG STEER on what level 1 is: "scale up reasoning reasonably" — perception, goal planning, cues and hopping are already in the model; level 1 only raises the reasoning slider so the LoRA gets enough data in the right distribution; "it's just fine-tuning"; "we've been wasting a lot of time on this level one and it's my fault … level one doesn't need to be complicated to close the loop"; Astra should know there is a deadline. (6) Environments: do not contaminate level-2/3 test environments by mining them for level-1 data; keep the best for level 3, decent ones for level 2, a large diverse pool for level-1 mining; level-1 environments may be ones the model already solves — the point is solving WITH richness (millennium-problem analogy: the learning is in the documented steps, not the solution). (7) Scale data creation, accumulation, curation and the number of data types. (8) Sleep: not every episode needs to be learnable-scale; several episodes per sleep; the compiler runs at sleep and will have a lot to consolidate. (9) Formatting will matter and must be tested, but the model already has it; "all we need is prompting our capabilities and training on good outputs" — context distillation plus curation. IDEA — a deterministic test of a whole sleep cycle's output for training-worthiness, then compile, train, and check the behaviour. Reading (Fable): a formalisation, thesis-level; he says he needs to fix the wording. Fable's crisp restatement of Rohin's ladder (message 80), in his terms, for him to correct:

| Level | One line | What is being trained / tested | Loop | Outcome signal | Status 2026-09-14 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 0 — Mechanism | With the correct inputs, the LoRA turns experience into behaviour and memory that persist. | Nothing learned; the mechanism is verified | none | acquisition / retention / erasure laws | DONE at DEV scope (dose, format, interleave, replay, loss-off) |
| 1 — Learn hard enough to change (open loop) | Scale the model's reasoning "reasonably" — more where it matters, less where it does not — so its own thought toward an action or an outcome is rich enough to be LoRA-learnable. | Fine-tuning on the child's own rich, outcome-successful thought, selected by a LoRA-aware evaluator under prompts that tell the model what to do | OPEN: prompt → act → evaluate → fit | held-instance behaviour change vs matched control | FIRST SUCCESS (SEQ-266/273: 2/32 → 30/32 → 16/16 held pairs, terse rows); rich rows in progress (math 29/32 vs 5/32; record yield 55/64) |
| 2 — Cohesion (closed loop + parenting) | Not just richer thought but the RIGHT richness: thinking that converges to learning across sleeps; the parent/evaluator critiques the closed loop's output so the loop actually works. | The closed loop itself: collect → compile → sleep → use, with a parent present during experience and reflection, absent at evaluation | CLOSED with feedback ("RL in the closed loop") | slope across sleeps on fresh tasks vs a frozen twin and an unparented twin; dependence on parenting decreasing | PREPARING (guided-loop CPU bridge) |
| 3 — Outcome convergence (generalised environments) | The model knows how to learn; now teach it to think so that its thinking improves action and capability toward long-term outcomes — hill-climbing worlds where richer reasoning alone plateaus. | The closed loop on held, hard, high-quality families (dozens), parent advising on learning values | CLOSED, long-horizon | capability slope on held families over many sleeps | NOT STARTED (families to be declared by the orchestrator) |
| 4 — Deployment | Unparented, on an unseen verifiable gym, against the non-learning twin. | nothing new; the test | CLOSED, no parent | H2: faster improvement than the twin, gap depends on continued sleep | NOT STARTED |

Rohin's RL framing per level: level 1 = fine-tune so the OPEN loop works; level 2 = evaluate the closed loop's output so the CLOSED loop works; level 3 = make the closed loop work for LONG-TERM outcomes; level 4 = deploy. Compiler: deterministic projection/format first (exists); learned "reasoning for learning" as a level-2/3 arm judged by the slope.

Reading (Fable; voice): (1) Dense guidance → more contention → fewer admits → META layers: two-pass ("I thought a bunch; let me think about what I thought") builds a thinking pyramid — that is how scaling of thought looks. (2) SCALING ADVICE (durable): scale by orders of magnitude, not by doubling — 5 → 50 → 500 → 5,000, then back off to 2,500; we do not know the upper bounds, so the search accelerates rather than creeps. (3) Meta-thinking ≈ consolidation: episodic compiling plus reflection that happens regardless; CONTEXT MANAGEMENT as a meta pass, recursive up and down the stack: under meaningful context pressure the model reflects on its context (not a refresh), distils what is valuable into a few tokens, decides what to keep, expands back — the model decides how much; this is learned later (level 3 / down the lineage); he will send his own context notes ("contacts"). (4) PROJECTION WITHDRAWN: no need to project down when compiling — reducing plasticity does it (lower-rank things get lost anyway). (5) COMPILER, last step = replay that ADDS and never removes: "these are the memories, let's add more … what do I want to learn" → a learning goal that affects the whole sequence and reinforces it — the model tries to form an abstract on its own. (6) QUESTION: why did the parented lanes not sleep? "We should sleep." (Answer: the gate admitted 0 rows under the short parent, and the loop skips the update when nothing is admitted; Fable relays his order that level-2 lanes sleep on what they have.) (7) PARENT: the most intelligent system available should parent (a copy of Astra), compared against a fresher/weaker parent; scale parental reasoning to a significant budget so the advice is actually good. (8) Level 2 is being formalised now; compiler and context window are the next things to think about; "we need richness happening WITH parenting to really prove the connection — let's go". Labels: STRONG STEER (order-of-magnitude scaling; guided lanes must sleep; parent = strongest model vs fresh, with scaled parental reasoning); IDEAS (meta-pass context management; additive replay compiler with a learning goal; projection dropped).

Reading (Fable; voice): (1) RULED — the compile gate is the wrong instrument for level 2: "we're not evaluating everything in compilation to leave things out; we're going to keep most of it if not all"; good AND bad learnings matter for self-learning (for simple rich behaviours richness alone suffices; parenting trains on both). (3) SLEEP IS PARENTED TOO: replay is a parented session in which the child thinks back over its previous parenting sessions; parenting sessions need strong richness. Two options — make the replay/dream portion more plastic than the experience portion, or give it more richness/data (possibly a sub-agent swarm enriching the replay); he prefers MATCHED plasticity; replay is long-horizon learned ("I replayed this last time as well") so long-horizon goals build in over sleeps — each sleep needs only ENOUGH richness not to be lost, the greater richness comes over the horizon. "Document that." (4) Richness ASAP ("should be easy"); once richness exists, "train the shit out of this loop"; replay happens right before or interleaved with the write; teaching (guided self-learning) begins once richness exists; LEVEL 1 IS DONE when teaching the model something changes its behaviour in the next cycle, even closed-loop. Use more GPUs. Labels: RULED (no selective gate at level 2 — keep all experience, good and bad, outcome-tagged); STEER (parented replay; matched plasticity; long-horizon replay; token-awareness parented later); CRITERION (level 1 done = taught → next-cycle behaviour change).

Reading (Fable; voice): Parenting is the hardest part because the child is no longer fed only success stories: the parent says "you should be learning this" and how to train itself on good data, and the child must then do it on its own in the closed loop, learning from good and bad — that is what gives it creativity and its own search. Slack by level: level 1 tight — more micromanaging because we care about the small-scale mechanism; level 2 = TRAINING WHEELS — let it do its thing a little, then catch wrong thinking ("are you thinking right?"); level 3 = more creativity — do not micromanage every thought ("then it's not thinking anymore") but do not withdraw entirely; push and pull; try different parenting styles; parenting is a full-time job. Context awareness: the parent looks at the child's context and what the child thinks matters, and teaches that — once the loop works. Close: get the richness done, level 1 out of the way, really rich; "we're ready for level two right now". Labels: STEER (parenting styles as arms; slack scales with level; parent content = learning direction, not only feedback); STRONG STEER (richness now; level 1 finished).

Reading (Fable; voice; "tropic" = Anthropic, "skiing" = scaling): (1) THESIS ORIGIN, "document this": the project began from the bitter lesson — more compute increases intelligence; the whole architecture is a bitter-lesson machine, and the developmental steps may be simpler than they look because we are only creating the first breath. LINEAGE = the scaling axis: the next generation does level 1 as one giant sleep on everything the last model had (post-training), then is parented at levels 2–3 and becomes a better parent itself; level 0 data feeds level 1; run it for a very long time; a level-4 lineage can be aimed at a specialty (e.g. GPU code design) and hill-climb there. (2) SCALING STANCE: like the few who believed in scaling laws more than anyone, once we have a semblance of richness we go all in: for level 1, start from the MOST scaling that is still useful and come down, not up; level 1 is done when the level-2 loop works; then make one great, contamination-free level 1 and forget it; we are nowhere near the compute maximum. (3) LEVEL 2 = a PARENTING SWARM: tons of agents on every GPU thinking and communicating to decide what good parenting is; continuous, non-stop parenting; Rohin at the head guiding; if something is not working, parent differently — never start from scratch; keep scaling; level 3 is level 2 with more environments and a higher climb. Labels: THESIS (document); STRONG STEER (start level-1 scaling from the top; level 2 = continuous parenting swarm on every GPU; crazy compute at level 2).

## Message 103 — 2026-09-15 ~05:06 UTC (typed fast; verbatim, questioning Fable's "rehearsal mix" caveat on more frequent sleeps)

hmmm so youre sayign that if we sleep more we have to start havign a mix beause were nto giving enough time for replay psosibly? what is the findemtaly differnce between spreading the traingin of sleep across? why does shorter sleep cle make it so you now need replay?

Reading (Fable): QUESTION, and a fair challenge to my wording. Fable's answer (logged in COORDINATION 05:1xZ): nothing fundamental changes in the total number of updates; what changes is the CONTENT MIX inside each sleep's batches. A sleep over 8 episodes interleaves rows from 8 episodes in every batch; a sleep over 1 episode takes the same updates in a block from one episode's rows. Blocked updates interfere with earlier learning more than interleaved ones (level 0: an unrehearsed habit was erased within ~16 narrow competing updates; mixing sources inside the batch preserved it). Mixing some old rows back in simply restores the interleaving that the big sleep gave for free — it is not that time is too short for replay. If a lane already rehearses (the continual trainer does), nothing changes. "Replay" here = rehearsal of old rows inside the batch, distinct from the replay-compile stage (the child's sleep-time reflection), which is unaffected by the sleep count.

Reading (Fable; "notes/nodes" = nodes, "Laura" = LoRA, "three hour" = three-arm): (1) RULED: sleep more often — try TWO episodes per sleep. Side effects of small sleeps are acceptable and even good in a young system: the child tries things, negative outcomes are kept as memory and trained as negatives, and close cycles let us test whether parenting actually works. Smaller sleeps = more behaviour change, easier to mould, more testing. While Rohin is awake, 2.5-hour cycles waste his time ("I need to be guiding things"); long chunks only while he sleeps. (2) Rehearsal ("replay episodes inside"): fine for now, but the old material should FADE — replay weighted toward frequently used items, decaying with age ("frequently used cache"); his original thought was no replay at the older stage: more efficient, at the cost of lower plasticity (the model changes less and focuses on what is already good) — that is the plasticity-down schedule. IDEA to test, not a ruling. (3) Acknowledges the readout block ("blocked by … a different [GPU]; okay good, the measure will land soon"). (4) RULED allocation: NOT three arms per cell — one three-arm baseline in total: one LoRA without parenting, one parented no-LoRA, and as many PARENTING arms as possible ("four parents and one no-LoRA parented"); "get those nodes something to do quickly, whatever is needed, keep going." (5) Wants results soon and a distilled answer on: branching updates, how parenting is going, whether the continual level-1 training is still running and constantly fed new data. Labels: RULED (two-episode sleeps; one three-arm baseline, rest parenting; no long cycles while awake); IDEA (fading rehearsal / no replay when older); QUESTIONS (branching, parenting, continual training).

Reading (Fable): design steer on the REPLAY SCHEDULE across the ladder — (1) during childhood (levels 2–3), keep rehearsal/replay of old rows: things change so often that important material would otherwise be lost; (2) the shipped model (level 4, deployment) has no replay, or probably none: plasticity is lower and the main things are already in the weights, so nothing needs re-presenting; (3) what the deployed model keeps is REFLECTION — the child's own thinking about its experience — which can happen at any time, including idle "white noise time" (an earlier idea), and reflection is taught by parenting. Labels: STEER (replay on in childhood, off at deployment; reflection replaces it); IDEA (idle-time reflection); consistent with the plasticity-down schedule (message 104) and the fading-rehearsal arm.

Reading (Fable): (1) Worse held accuracy is NOT the problem — we care about behaviour SHAPE, not output. (2) A model he consulted suggested prompting some META capability into the behaviour (idea). (3) QUESTION: what is the actual behaviour — if it thinks differently and does worse without branching, "where is the thinking actually going?" (4) CORRECTION of my operationalisation of BRANCHING: it does not mean multiple hypotheses or pure decision branching (two worked methods). Branching = while on a chain of thought, going down other forms and coming back — associative side-paths that later inform the answer (the car example: notice a car → what would it cost → back to the main path → the person in the car → other cars → when asked which sells best, the side-observations decide). Checks and judgments are a form of branching; anything that is not a linear A-B-C-D-E-F-G chain counts; a "trailing mind", "curiosity in a sentence" — think about many things so you act more, learn more, get more out of each situation. (5) "Maybe you're overstating the failure." (6) Standing warning: early on we may call negative what is positive, and count irrelevant things as positives; he will check us on that. Labels: STEER (behaviour shape over output; branching = mycelial side-paths incl. checks/judgments, measured as departures-and-returns from the main chain, not as second methods); QUESTION (where does the thinking go); IDEA (prompt a meta capability); CORRECTION to Fable's P65 measure.
